--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion uses a 2-state FSM:
- State 0: Attraction (move toward nearby robots) with transition to state 1 when neighbors BELOW 5 (sigmoid steepness 9.01) — so when isolated, go to GoToColor
- State 1: GoToColor (blue=3, the target strip) with transition back to state 0 when neighbors ABOVE 3 (sigmoid steepness 17.4) — when grouped, do attraction

This makes sense: robots go toward blue walls to reach the target strip, and when they have enough neighbors (already in the group), they do attraction to stay aggregated.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep the champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Exploit the champion with small parameter tweaks
3. Explore different topologies

**Exploitation variants of champion:**
- Vary att0 (3.75 → 3.0, 4.5, 5.0)
- Vary clr1 (3=blue is correct for target, keep it)
- Vary neighbor thresholds (p0x0: 5→4, 6; p1x0: 3→2, 4)
- Vary sigmoid steepness
- Add a 3rd state for floor detection

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: explore + go-to-blue + stop-on-black
- GoToColor blue directly with floor-based transitions
- Spiral to find the target strip, then GoToColor

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. att0 = 3.0, rest same
3. att0 = 4.5, rest same
4. p0x0 threshold = 4 (fewer neighbors needed to switch to GoToColor)
5. p0x0 threshold = 6, p1x0 threshold = 4
6. w0x0 = 15.0 (sharper sigmoid), w1x0 = 10.0
7. Add second transition from state 0 on floor black (C=0) → go to state 1
8. 3-state: Exploration → GoToColor(blue) → Attraction, with floor-based transitions
9. Pure GoToColor blue (1-state) — simple baseline
10. GoToColor blue + stop when on black floor
11. 2-state: Exploration + GoToColor blue with floor triggers
12. 3-state with repulsion away from green, attraction to blue
13. 2-state: GoToColor blue → FlockCenter
14. 3-state variant with spiral search

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The new champion (1899.31) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue=3) → transitions to state 1 when neighbors ABOVE 4 (steepness 12) OR floor is BLACK (p=0.95)
- State 1: FlockCenter(gain=50) → transitions back to state 0 when neighbors BELOW 3 (steepness 10) OR floor is GRAY (p=0.8)

This is elegant: robots go toward the blue wall (target strip), and once they're grouped (many neighbors) or on the black floor (target strip), they flock together. When isolated or on gray (not in target), they return to GoToColor.

The second best (1916.59) uses Attraction + GoToColor(blue) with similar logic.

**Exploitation of champion (1899.31):**
1. Vary fc1 (50 → 40, 60, 70)
2. Vary neighbor thresholds (p0x0: 4→3,5; p1x0: 3→2,4)
3. Vary sigmoid steepness
4. Vary p0x1 (0.95 → 0.9, 1.0) and p1x1 (0.8 → 0.7, 0.9)
5. Add 3rd transition to states

**Exploitation of 2nd best (1916.59):**
- Vary att0, thresholds

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor → FlockCenter → Attraction
- GoToColor blue + Stop on black + FlockCenter

## Reasoning

The new champion (1665.39) differs from the previous best in key ways:
- State 0: neighbor threshold ABOVE 5 (was 4), steepness 12, black floor p=0.9 (was 0.95)
- State 1: fc1=60, neighbor threshold BELOW 4 (was 3), steepness 12 (was 10), gray floor p=0.8, black floor p=0.85

So the pattern is: higher neighbor threshold to switch to FlockCenter, higher steepness for the return condition too. The black floor probability in state 0 dropped slightly (0.9 vs 0.95).

Key observations:
- fc1=60,70,80,90 all performed well (scores ~1665-1818), with 60 winning on p0x0=5 combo
- 3-state machines underperformed vs 2-state
- 4 transitions in state 1 hurt

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Vary fc1 around 60 with the champion's exact topology (55, 65, 75)
3. Vary p0x0 neighbor threshold (5→6) and p1x0 (4→5)
4. Vary steepness w0x0 and w1x0
5. Combine p0x0=5 with fc1=90 (mixing best features from #1 and #2)
6. Try p0x1=0.85 and p1x2=0.8 (slight tweaks)
7. Try adding a 4th transition to state 0 (camera sees blue → go to flock)

**Exploration:**
- Try GoToColor blue + Attraction (instead of FlockCenter) with champion topology params

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (1665.39) remains unchanged after two rounds. The scores from round 6 are notably higher than the champion (best non-champion is 1851), suggesting we may be near a local optimum with this topology.

Key observations:
- The champion topology is very well-tuned: GoToColor(blue) → FlockCenter with neighbor/floor transitions
- fc1=90 performed WORSE (2118) in round 6 despite performing well (1686) in round 5 — this suggests high variance
- Fixed-probability transition in state 1 (c=5, p=0.02) hurt badly
- Swapping state order (FlockCenter first) hurt
- Multiple machines scored the SAME (1887.64) — likely identical evaluations

The champion's key parameters: p0x0=5, w0x0=12, p0x1=0.9, fc1=60, p1x0=4, w1x0=12, p1x1=0.8, p1x2=0.85

Given we're stuck, I should:
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Fine-tune around champion with small perturbations
3. Explore genuinely different approaches to escape local optima

**Fine-tuning:**
- Try w0x0=12 with p0x0=5 but vary p1x2 (0.82, 0.87)
- Try fc1=55, 65 (between successful values)
- Try w1x0=14, 16
- Cross p1x0=5 (from best #1) with fc1=60

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor → FlockCenter → stop on black (different from previous 3-state attempts)
- Try GoToColor(blue) with Attraction instead of FlockCenter, using champion-like transitions
- Try a completely different approach: Exploration + GoToColor blue with floor sensing

## Reasoning

New champion: fc1=55 (1643.12), beating fc1=60 (1665.39). The sweet spot for fc seems to be around 55-65. The topology is very stable now.

Key observations:
- fc1=55 best, fc1=65 second (1673), fc1=60 third (1685)
- w0x0=12, w1x0=12 consistently best (w=14,20 hurt)
- p1x2=0.88 slightly better than 0.85 in one case (1685 vs champion 1643... actually champion has 0.85 so 0.88 didn't beat it)
- 3-state approaches consistently fail
- The topology is very locked in: 2-state, GoToColor(blue) → FlockCenter

Fine-tuning focus:
1. fc1 around 55: try 50, 52, 57, 58
2. p1x2 around 0.85: try 0.83, 0.86, 0.87, 0.88
3. p0x1 around 0.9: try 0.88, 0.92
4. w1x0 around 12: try 11, 13 (small steps since 14 hurt)
5. p1x0=4 vs 5 with fc1=55
6. Cross best features: fc1=55 with p1x2=0.88

For exploration (to escape local optima):
- Try a completely fresh approach with floor-sensing as primary signal
- Try GoToColor(blue) as single state (simplest possible)
- Try GoAwayColor(green) which should push toward blue side

## Reasoning

This is the final round (10/10). The champion (1641.69) has fc1=52, p0x1=0.9, p1x1=0.8, p1x2=0.85 and hasn't been beaten in rounds 8-9.

Key observations from round 9:
- p0x1=0.88 (1742) performed second best — slightly lower than 0.9
- p1x2=0.86 (1751) performed third — slightly higher than 0.85
- fc1=48 (1770) and fc1=54 (1783) both worse than fc1=52
- p1x1=0.78 with fc1=52 (1797) worse than 0.8
- p1x2=0.87 (1876) and fc1=53 (1891) hurt
- fc1=50 with p1x1=0.78 (2031) hurt

The champion is very stable at fc1=52, w0x0=12, w1x0=12, p0x0=5, p0x1=0.9, p1x0=4, p1x1=0.8, p1x2=0.85.

For the final round, I should:
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Try fine perturbations that haven't been tested: p0x1=0.89, 0.91; p1x2=0.84, 0.85 (already best); fc1=51, 52 (champion); 
3. Cross p0x1=0.88 with p1x2=0.86 (combine two second-best features)
4. Try p0x0=5 with w0x0=11 or 13 (steepness tweak)
5. Some exploration: try a 3-state or different topology as a last chance

Given the tight convergence, I'll focus almost entirely on fine exploitation with a couple of exploratory machines:

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 13 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.53 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
